  it is phrase-indexed. The real cause is that **Trial registration** is a structured abstract
  section label carried verbatim by every registered trial. Test the two forms against each
  other before concluding anything about a phrase.
- **Run `--counts` before harvesting anything.** It costs a few minutes and it is what exposed
  the above. A block sized after it has been downloaded is a block downloaded twice.
- **A tier B topic phrase retrieves studies that did the thing, not studies about it.** No
  boolean query separates them. That is a classification problem for a later stage, not a query
  to be tuned.
- **NCBI returns at most 9999 ids per query.** `collect_ids` bisects the publication-date range
  recursively until every part is under the ceiling, and records any single day still over it in
  `gaps` rather than truncating silently. The sibling project stops at month granularity and can
  truncate without saying so; do not copy that behavior back.
- **The efetch cache is keyed on the content of each batch, not its position.** Position keying
  is wrong the moment a query changes, and the failure is silent because every printed count
  comes from esearch and stays correct while the parsed records go stale.

## Sibling projects

`TTE-open-problems` and `ITC-open-problems` are the same author's audited catalogs of open
methodological problems, and this repository's harvester, theme and CI are adapted from TTE.
Worth reading before building a new stage here, particularly `build/adjudicate.mjs`, which turns
independent auditor opinions into a published verdict by a fixed rule, and its CLAUDE.md section
on how generated study code fails. Every defect it lists was silent.

Do not copy `render_site.mjs`, `adjudicate.mjs` or `build/studies/` wholesale. They encode TTE's
eighteen category codes and its problem-registry schema, neither of which exists here.
---
title: "How this was done"
subtitle: "The search protocol, the decisions taken, and what they cost"
toc: true
---

This project studies transparency in research. It would be indefensible for the project itself
to be opaque, so every query, every count and every scope decision is recorded here, including
the ones that went wrong.

## Databases and dates

PubMed and PMC, through the NCBI E-utilities. PMC is searched as well as PubMed because PMC
carries records PubMed does not index: author manuscripts, some non-MEDLINE journals, and
deposited preprints. Searches were run on 2026-09-16. No language, date or article-type limit
was applied.

The harvester is `build/lit/search.py`. It is the only route by which a record enters the
corpus, and it writes a machine-readable log of every query it issued to
`data/lit/search-log.json`.

## The two tiers

The field can be searched two ways and they retrieve different things.

**Tier A** is the field naming itself: a paper that describes its own work as meta-research,
metascience, research on research, or a meta-epidemiological study. Precision is close to
total, because no other kind of paper uses those words about itself.

**Tier B** is the field by subject matter: reporting quality, data sharing, retraction,
registration, peer review, and the rest. Tier B retrieves two populations that no boolean query
separates. A search for `"data sharing"` returns surveys of how often trials share data
alongside trials that happened to share theirs, and only the first kind is meta-research.

**Only tier A has been harvested.** Tier B has been sized and the counts are published below,
but nothing from it is in the corpus. That decision is revisited once the tier A map has been
read.

## Tier A: the query

Each phrase is searched in title, abstract and author keyword fields, and the phrases are
combined with OR.

```
"meta-research"      "metaresearch"           "meta research"
"metascience"        "meta-science"           "research on research"
"science of science" "meta-epidemiological"   "meta-epidemiology"
```

| database | hits | records retrieved |
|---|---|---|
| PubMed | 1805 | 1805 |
| PMC | 694 | 694 |

Deduplicated on PMID, then DOI, then PMCID, then normalized title: **1830 unique records**.
The overlap is large, as expected; 669 PMC records were already present from PubMed and 25 were
new.

## Tier B: the counts, and why they are not the corpus

Measured 2026-09-16 in PubMed. The third column is the same block ANDed with a
study-of-literature qualifier described below.

| block | phrases cover | raw hits | qualified |
|---|---|---|---|
| `reg` | registration, outcome switching, publication bias | 189588 | 8205 |
| `biblio` | authorship, citation, metrics | 17419 | 16265 |
| `report` | reporting quality, guideline adherence | 16657 | 2395 |
| `datashare` | data and code sharing, FAIR | 15418 | 767 |
| `retract` | retraction, misconduct, integrity | 11128 | 637 |
| `repro` | reproducibility, replication | 6421 | 241 |
| `oa` | open access, preprints, predatory publishing | 4475 | 1050 |
| `peerrev` | peer review | 2910 | 325 |
| `coi` | conflicts of interest, funding | 1387 | not measured |
| `waste` | research waste, prioritization | 1061 | not measured |
| `stats` | p-hacking, spin, questionable practices | 545 | not measured |
| `equity` | equity and representation | 326 | not measured |
| `aitext` | generative AI in research writing | 106 | not measured |

### The 189588

The `reg` block first returned 189588 records, five sixths of everything tier B retrieved, from
the single phrase `"trial registration"`. The obvious explanation was wrong. The phrase was not
being broken into words and ANDed by PubMed; it is phrase-indexed, and the quoted form returns
fewer records than `trial AND registration` does. The real cause is that **Trial registration**
is the label of a section in a structured abstract, so every registered trial in PubMed carries
the phrase verbatim. It identifies trials that were registered and never studies of
registration.

The phrase has been removed from the block. `build/lit/search.py` records why, next to the
block it was removed from, so it is not reintroduced by someone who only sees that the block
looks thin.

`"registered report"` was removed for a related reason: the singular returns 5806 records and
the plural 192, because the singular also matches any sentence about a report that was
registered. Only the plural is kept.

### The qualifier

For the contaminated blocks, ANDing the topic with a set of phrases that name the object of
study separates a study of the literature from a study of patients. A study of data sharing
examines published articles; a trial that shared its data examines people.

```
"published articles"  "published studies"    "published trials"
"journal articles"    "publications"         "sample of articles"
"cross-sectional study of"                   "sample of trials"
"we searched PubMed"  "we searched MEDLINE"  "we searched Embase"
"research articles"   "abstracts of"         "meta-epidemiological"
```

It is effective and it is lossy. A meta-research paper that describes its sample as "we
examined 300 trials" carries none of these phrases and would be lost. Both numbers are
therefore recorded, so any qualified corpus is reported against the raw count it was drawn
from rather than presented as the whole field.

## Retrieval limits, and why nothing was silently truncated

NCBI returns at most 9999 identifiers for one query. The sibling project this harvester is
adapted from partitions an oversized query by year and then by month and stops there, so a
single month above the ceiling truncates without saying so. Meta-research blocks are large and
concentrated in recent years, which is where that would happen.

The partitioner here bisects the publication-date range recursively until every part is under
the ceiling. When even a single day exceeds it, the day is recorded in `gaps` in the search log
and printed as `TRUNCATED`, rather than passed off as complete. The tier A harvest needed no
partitioning: both queries were under the ceiling.

## Labelling

Topic labels are assigned in `build/lit/describe.py` by matching the tier B phrase vocabulary
against each record's title, abstract and author keywords. Matching is case-insensitive,
treats a hyphen and a space as equivalent, as PubMed does, and requires whole-token boundaries
so that `spin` does not match `spinal`.

Two vocabularies are involved and conflating them caused the one error this project has so far
published. The **search** vocabulary is tuned for precision against all of PubMed, where a
phrase as broad as `"peer review"` retrieves every paper that mentions having been peer
reviewed. The **labelling** vocabulary applies inside a corpus that is already meta-research,
where that same narrowness only hides topics: the search phrases labelled peer review on 11
records while 159 of them mention it, and generative AI on 1 while 66 mention a language model.
A conclusion drawn from those counts, that the field does not study its own quality control,
was published on the landscape page and was false.

Broad labelling phrases now live in `LABEL_EXTRA` in `build/lit/describe.py`, applied only to
records already retrieved and never to a query. The corrected counts are on
[the landscape](landscape.qmd), which also states what the earlier version claimed.

A label means the record uses a topic's vocabulary. It does not mean a human, or a model,
judged the record to be about that topic. No model has read any record in this corpus. Records
can carry several labels; 1819 of 1830 carry at least one and 11 carry none.

## What this corpus is not

- **It is not the meta-research literature.** It is the part of it that names itself. Work
  published before roughly 2015, when the vocabulary settled, largely does not appear even when
  it is unmistakably meta-research by subject.
- **It is not screened.** Every record retrieved by the tier A query is in, including editorials,
  comments and letters. Publication types are reported on the landscape page so a reader can see
  how much of the corpus that is.
- **It carries no preprints beyond what PMC deposits.** Europe PMC indexes preprint servers
  under a source PubMed has no equivalent for. Adding that supplement is a known gap, not an
  oversight.
- **Nothing here has been peer reviewed yet.** The review process is described in
  [the pipeline](index.qmd#the-pipeline) and has not run.

## Reproducing it

```bash
python3 build/lit/search.py --check          # assert the parser still works
python3 build/lit/search.py --counts         # size every block, download nothing
python3 build/lit/search.py --tier A         # harvest, resumable, cached
python3 build/lit/describe.py                # derive the map
python3 build/render_site.py                 # write landscape.qmd and corpus.qmd
quarto render                                # -> docs/
```

`data/lit/records.jsonl` is tracked, so the corpus can be re-described without re-harvesting.
The XML cache is not tracked; it is 101 MB and re-fetchable.

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""OpenAlex probes shared by P03 and P06.

P03 needs citation counts on the tier A corpus. P06 needs to know how many of those records
have a preprint version. Both answers come from the same records, so both are fetched in one
pass of about 37 calls rather than twice.

Output: data/lit/openalex.jsonl, one line per matched record, and a printed summary.

Usage: python3 studies/_shared/openalex_probe.py
"""

import collections
import json
import os
import sys
import time
import urllib.parse
import urllib.request

HERE = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))
ROOT = os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(HERE))
# Which corpus to resolve. Passing a stem rather than copying this file: a sed-edited copy
# in a temp directory resolved ROOT from its own location and looked for the corpus in the
# temp tree, which is the obvious failure and took a run to notice.
STEM = sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv) > 1 else "records"
CORPUS = os.path.join(ROOT, "data", "lit", f"{STEM}.jsonl")
OUT = os.path.join(ROOT, "data", "lit", f"openalex-{STEM}.jsonl")
SUMMARY = os.path.join(ROOT, "data", "lit", f"openalex-{STEM}-summary.json")
MAILTO = "ahmad.pub@gmail.com"
UA = f"autometaresearch/1.0 (mailto:{MAILTO})"

# OpenAlex asks for a mailto and gives the polite pool in return. 50 ids per filter is the
# documented ceiling for an OR list.
BATCH = 50
SELECT = ("id,doi,ids,publication_year,cited_by_count,type,"
          "primary_location,locations,open_access,authorships,referenced_works_count")


def get(url, tries=4):
    for attempt in range(tries):
        try:
            req = urllib.request.Request(url, headers={"User-Agent": UA})
            with urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=90) as r:
                return json.loads(r.read().decode("utf8"))
        except Exception as e:  # noqa: BLE001
            if attempt == tries - 1:
                raise
            print(f"    retry {attempt + 1} after {e}", file=sys.stderr)
            time.sleep(2 ** attempt)
    return {}


def main():
    records = [json.loads(l) for l in open(CORPUS, encoding="utf8")]
    pmids = [r["pmid"] for r in records if r["pmid"]]
    print(f"{len(pmids)} PMIDs to resolve", file=sys.stderr)

    out = []
    for i in range(0, len(pmids), BATCH):
        batch = pmids[i:i + BATCH]
        q = urllib.parse.urlencode({
            "filter": "pmid:" + "|".join(batch),
            "select": SELECT, "per-page": 100, "mailto": MAILTO,
        })
        d = get(f"https://api.openalex.org/works?{q}")
        out.extend(d.get("results", []))
        print(f"  {min(i + BATCH, len(pmids))}/{len(pmids)}", end="\r", file=sys.stderr)
        time.sleep(0.15)
    print(file=sys.stderr)

    with open(OUT, "w", encoding="utf8") as fh:
        for w in out:
            fh.write(json.dumps(w, ensure_ascii=False) + "\n")

    cites = sorted(w.get("cited_by_count", 0) for w in out)
    # A preprint version means a location whose source is a repository, other than the
    # primary. PMC is a repository too, so a bare repository count would say "almost all of
    # them" and mean nothing; the preprint servers are named explicitly.
    PREPRINT = ("biorxiv", "medrxiv", "arxiv", "ssrn", "osf", "preprints.org",
                "research square", "zenodo", "psyarxiv", "metaarxiv")
    with_preprint, by_server = 0, collections.Counter()
    for w in out:
        names = set()
        for loc in (w.get("locations") or []):
            src = (loc.get("source") or {})
            nm = (src.get("display_name") or "").lower()
            if any(p in nm for p in PREPRINT):
                names.add(nm)
        if names:
            with_preprint += 1
            for nm in names:
                by_server[nm] += 1

    oa = collections.Counter((w.get("open_access") or {}).get("oa_status") for w in out)
    n = len(out)
    summary = {
        "pmids_queried": len(pmids),
        "matched_in_openalex": n,
        "match_rate": round(n / max(len(pmids), 1), 4),
        "citations_median": cites[n // 2] if n else 0,
        "citations_mean": round(sum(cites) / max(n, 1), 2),
        "citations_zero": sum(1 for c in cites if c == 0),
        "citations_p90": cites[int(n * 0.9)] if n else 0,
        "citations_max": cites[-1] if n else 0,
        "records_with_a_preprint_version": with_preprint,
        "preprint_rate": round(with_preprint / max(n, 1), 4),
        "top_preprint_servers": by_server.most_common(8),
        "oa_status": dict(oa),
    }
    json.dump(summary, open(SUMMARY, "w"), indent=1)
    for k, v in summary.items():
        print(f"  {k}: {v}")
    print(f"\n-> {OUT}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

def _text(node):
    """Flatten an element's text, including markup children like <i> and <sup>."""
    return re.sub(r"\s+", " ", "".join(node.itertext())).strip() if node is not None else ""


def article_ids(art, paths):
    """The record's own identifiers, from its ArticleIdList and nowhere else.

    A blanket art.iter("ArticleId") also walks ReferenceList and CommentsCorrectionsList, so
    the DOI and PMCID that come back belong to the last work the article happens to cite.
    Restrict to the declared paths.
    """
    ids = {}
    for path in paths:
        for i in art.findall(path):
            t, v = i.get("IdType"), (i.text or "").strip()
            if t and v and t not in ids:
                ids[t] = v
    pmc = ids.get("pmc", "")
    if pmc and not pmc.upper().startswith("PMC"):
        ids["pmc"] = "PMC" + pmc
    return ids


def parse_pubmed(xml):
    root = ET.fromstring(xml)
    out = []
    for art in root.iter("PubmedBookArticle"):
        rec = parse_pubmed_book(art)
        if rec:
            out.append(rec)
    for art in root.iter("PubmedArticle"):
        cit = art.find("MedlineCitation")
        a = cit.find(".//Article")
        if a is None:
            continue
        ids = article_ids(art, ("PubmedData/ArticleIdList/ArticleId",))
        j = a.find("Journal")
        year = ""
        for tag in (".//ArticleDate/Year", ".//JournalIssue/PubDate/Year",
                    ".//JournalIssue/PubDate/MedlineDate"):
            v = _text(a.find(tag)) or _text(cit.find(tag))
            if v:
                year = v[:4]
                break
        abstract = " ".join(
            (seg.get("Label", "") + ": " if seg.get("Label") else "") + _text(seg)
            for seg in a.iter("AbstractText")
        ).strip()
        out.append({
            "pmid": _text(cit.find("PMID")),
            "pmcid": ids.get("pmc", ""),
            "doi": ids.get("doi", "").lower(),
            "title": _text(a.find("ArticleTitle")),
            "abstract": abstract,
            "journal": _text(j.find("Title")) if j is not None else "",
            "journal_abbrev": _text(j.find("ISOAbbreviation")) if j is not None else "",
            "year": year,
            "pubtypes": [_text(p) for p in a.iter("PublicationType")],
            "keywords": [_text(k) for k in cit.iter("Keyword")],
            "mesh": [_text(m.find("DescriptorName")) for m in cit.iter("MeshHeading")],
            "authors": [
                (_text(au.find("LastName")) + " " + _text(au.find("Initials"))).strip()
                for au in a.iter("Author")
            ][:12],
            "language": _text(a.find(".//Language")),
        })
    return out


def parse_pubmed_book(art):
    """Bookshelf records, which PubMed returns as PubmedBookArticle.

    Worth parsing rather than dropping: several of the standing guidance documents this
    field cites, including NICE and Cochrane methods series, are indexed here and nowhere
    else in PubMed.
    """
    cit = art.find("BookDocument")
    if cit is None:
        return None
    ids = article_ids(art, ("BookDocument/ArticleIdList/ArticleId",
                            "PubmedBookData/ArticleIdList/ArticleId"))
    book = cit.find("Book")
    title = _text(cit.find("ArticleTitle")) or _text(book.find("BookTitle") if book is not None else None)
    abstract = " ".join(
        (seg.get("Label", "") + ": " if seg.get("Label") else "") + _text(seg)
        for seg in cit.iter("AbstractText")
    ).strip()
    return {
        "pmid": _text(cit.find("PMID")),
        "pmcid": ids.get("pmc", ""),
        "doi": ids.get("doi", "").lower(),
        "title": title,
        "abstract": abstract,
        "journal": _text(book.find("BookTitle")) if book is not None else "",
        "journal_abbrev": _text(book.find("Publisher/PublisherName")) if book is not None else "",
        "year": (_text(book.find("PubDate/Year")) if book is not None else "")[:4],
        "pubtypes": [_text(p) for p in cit.iter("PublicationType")] or ["Book Chapter"],
        "keywords": [_text(k) for k in cit.iter("Keyword")],
        "mesh": [],
        "authors": [
            (_text(au.find("LastName")) + " " + _text(au.find("Initials"))).strip()
            for au in cit.iter("Author")
        ][:12],
        "language": _text(cit.find(".//Language")),
    }


def parse_pmc(xml):
    """PMC efetch returns full JATS; take only the front matter we need."""
    out = []
    root = ET.fromstring(xml)
    for art in list(root.iter("article")) + list(root.iter("book")):
        meta = art.find(".//article-meta")
        if meta is None:
            continue
        ids = {i.get("pub-id-type"): (i.text or "").strip() for i in meta.iter("article-id")}
        # PMC's own JATS labels the accession "pmcid"; "pmc" appears in publisher-deposited
        # files. Take either, and always store it prefixed.
        pmcid = ids.get("pmcid") or ids.get("pmc") or ""
        if pmcid and not pmcid.upper().startswith("PMC"):
            pmcid = "PMC" + pmcid
        jm = art.find(".//journal-meta")
        jtitle = _text(jm.find(".//journal-title")) if jm is not None else ""
        abbrev = ""
        if jm is not None:
            for jid in jm.iter("journal-id"):
                if jid.get("journal-id-type") in ("nlm-ta", "iso-abbrev"):
                    abbrev = _text(jid)
                    break
        year = ""
        for pd in meta.iter("pub-date"):
            y = _text(pd.find("year"))
            if y:
                year = y
                break
        out.append({
            "pmid": ids.get("pmid", ""),
            "pmcid": pmcid,
            "doi": ids.get("doi", "").lower(),
            "title": _text(meta.find(".//article-title")),
            "abstract": " ".join(_text(ab) for ab in meta.iter("abstract")).strip(),
            "journal": jtitle,
            "journal_abbrev": abbrev,
            "year": year,
            "pubtypes": [art.get("article-type", "")],
            "keywords": [_text(k) for k in meta.iter("kwd")],
            "mesh": [],
            "authors": [
                (_text(c.find("surname")) + " " + _text(c.find("given-names"))).strip()
                for c in meta.iter("name")
            ][:12],
            "language": "",
        })
    return out

    """Fetch records in batches by explicit id, caching each batch.

/bin/zsh -c 'rtk proxy git show ed7bb06f1ca18cbbb8337128caaff6eac3f95cab:proposals/P03-bad-news-citation.qmd' in /Users/choxos/Documents/GitHub/autometaresearch

---
title: "Is bad news cited more?"
description: "Whether meta-research reporting poor practice accrues more citations than meta-research reporting good practice."
id: P03
status: proposed
proposed_by: "Claude Opus 5"
date: 2026-09-16
reviewed: none
unit: "Meta-research articles with OpenAlex citation counts"
data_source: "OpenAlex; the project corpus"
categories: [runnable, biblio, incentives]
---

::: {.callout-note appearance="simple"}
Proposed by Claude Opus 5 on 2026-09-16. Not yet reviewed. This page is a protocol and no
analysis has been run.
:::

## The question

Meta-research reports on how well other research is done, and the answer is usually that it is
done badly. If findings of failure are rewarded with citations more than findings that a
practice is adequate, the field has an incentive gradient pointing at one conclusion, and that
gradient would be invisible in any individual study.

This is publication bias asked about the field that studies publication bias, and asked at the
citation stage rather than the acceptance stage.

## Hypothesis

Among meta-research articles, those concluding that the practice studied is inadequate accrue
more citations than those concluding it is adequate, after adjustment for age, journal and
topic.

**What would falsify it.** An incidence rate ratio whose interval contains 1. Given a corpus of
1830 and citation counts that are highly skewed, the design is powered for a moderate effect
and not a subtle one; the detectable effect size is computed and published before the run.

## Unit of analysis

One meta-research article. All 1830 corpus records are eligible. OpenAlex matched 50 of 50
sampled PMIDs in a single call, so coverage is effectively complete and the whole corpus
resolves in about 37 calls.

Articles published in 2025 and 2026 are retained but age is modeled rather than trimmed, since
excluding recent work would drop 76 percent of the corpus.

## The hard part, stated plainly

The exposure is the direction of the article's conclusion, and no rule extracts it from an
abstract. This is the one proposal of the three whose exposure requires a model to read text,
and that is a methodological liability rather than a convenience.

The design therefore treats classification as a measurement instrument that must be validated
before it is used:

1. A stratified random sample of 300 abstracts is classified by hand, by the author, into
   inadequate, adequate, mixed or not applicable. This is the reference standard.
2. Two models from different providers classify the same 300, blind to the hand labels and to
   each other.
3. Agreement is reported as Cohen's kappa against the reference standard and between the models.
   **If neither model reaches kappa 0.7 against the hand labels, the study stops and the
   negative methodological result is published.** That outcome is a finding about whether
   language models can read a conclusion direction, and it is worth publishing.
4. Only if the threshold is met does classification extend to the remaining records, with the
   measured misclassification rate carried into a sensitivity analysis.

The stopping rule is written here, before any classification has been run, because a threshold
chosen after seeing the agreement is not a threshold.

## Analysis

In R. Negative binomial regression of citation count on conclusion direction.

```r
glm.nb(cited_by_count ~ direction + poly(years_since_pub, 2) +
         topic_block + (journal), data = d)
```

Age enters as a quadratic rather than an offset because citation accrual is not linear in time
and the corpus spans 1965 to 2026. A quantile regression on the upper citation deciles is
fitted alongside, since a mean effect and a tail effect answer different questions and the
incentive argument lives in the tail.

## Novelty

Predictors of citation rates have been reviewed (PMID 41265666) and citation bias is an
established topic. What is absent is the direction of a study's own conclusion as the exposure,
inside meta-research, where the conclusion direction is a property of the field's product
rather than of a clinical result.

## What it costs

37 OpenAlex calls, free and unauthenticated. Hand classification of 300 abstracts is the real
cost and it falls on the author, not on a model. Two model classification passes.

## Known threats to the result

- **Reverse causation is not addressable here.** A widely cited article may attract a negative
  framing in the abstract rather than the other way round.
- **Topic confounds direction.** Some topics almost never produce an adequate verdict, so
  adjustment for topic block may absorb the effect being measured. Reported both adjusted and
  unadjusted, with the difference discussed rather than resolved.
- **Self-citation and field size.** Not adjusted for in the primary model. OpenAlex exposes the
  data to do it and it is a stated secondary analysis.

    Write protocols and designs for every study, plus five new questions
    
    Nine documents now, each written to the sibling projects' design standard with a
    threats table, a probes table naming what each probe computes and what result
    would change the design, and a status that says what it actually is. Five are at
    protocol stage with their feasibility computed; three are designs whose probes
    have not run; one is an agenda item.
    
    The probes did the work they exist to do.
    
    P02: 83 per cent of references carry a DOI, not the 62 per cent estimated from
    three articles, and 83 post-retraction citations across 70 articles are enough to
    model. 107 of the 190 raw matches are pre-retraction citations, which a study
    counting matches alone would have reported as more than twice the true rate. A
    first version of the coverage figure came out at 1.41, impossible, because the
    plain-text fallback regex matches several times inside one reference and the
    numerator was not a subset of the denominator.
    
    P03: 1790 of 1805 tier A records resolve in OpenAlex, median 8 citations against
    a mean of 36, which is why the model is negative binomial with a quantile
    regression alongside.
    
    P05: 721 locators from 431 statements, and 88.7 per cent resolve on a sample of
    150. Two failures were a Unicode encoding bug in the requester rather than link
    rot, and counting a tool failure as a property of the world is exactly what the
    study is about, so it is recorded and must be fixed before the full run.
    
    P06: the linkage probe returned 141 pairs. Section 4 had already written the rule
    that 200 or more means a full protocol, 80 to 200 a reduced one, and under 80 an
    agenda item, so the ordinal outcome is cut to a binary McNemar comparison. The
    rule was applied as written rather than renegotiated.
    
    Five new questions: whether statements graded actionable actually resolve;
    whether conclusions soften between preprint and journal; whether two
    meta-research studies measuring the same practice agree, which the corpus search
    returns zero prior studies for; whether meta-research follows the reporting
    guidelines it audits others by; and whether documenting a problem changes it.
    
    A sixth was dropped for lack of novelty and the agenda says so: journal
    transparency policy against the transparency of the meta-research it publishes is
    covered by PMID 37433624.
    
    Two claims contradicted their own numbers and both are corrected on the page
    rather than edited away. P06 asserted 108 was "below the 80 the rule requires",
    which it is not, so the biomedical restriction becomes the primary analysis
    instead of the excuse for keeping arXiv. The agenda said four documents are at
    protocol stage when five are.

-## The question
+## 1. The question
 
 Meta-research reports on how well other research is done, and the answer is usually that it is
 done badly. If findings of failure are rewarded with citations more than findings that a
-practice is adequate, the field has an incentive gradient pointing at one conclusion, and that
-gradient would be invisible in any individual study.
+practice is adequate, the field faces an incentive gradient pointing at one conclusion, and
+that gradient would be invisible within any single study.
 
 This is publication bias asked about the field that studies publication bias, and asked at the
-citation stage rather than the acceptance stage.
+citation stage rather than at acceptance.
 
-## Hypothesis
+## 2. Hypothesis
 
 Among meta-research articles, those concluding that the practice studied is inadequate accrue
-more citations than those concluding it is adequate, after adjustment for age, journal and
-topic.
+more citations than those concluding it is adequate, adjusted for age, journal and topic.
+
+**Primary null.** The incidence rate ratio for conclusion direction is 1.
+
+**What would falsify it.** An interval containing 1.
+
+## 3. Design
+
+Cross-sectional study of published articles with a count outcome.

-1830 and citation counts that are highly skewed, the design is powered for a moderate effect
-and not a subtle one; the detectable effect size is computed and published before the run.

+## 6. Exposure, and the gate
 
-## Analysis
+The exposure is the direction of the article's own conclusion, and no rule extracts it from an
+abstract. This is the one study of the set whose exposure needs a model to read text, and that
+is a liability rather than a convenience.
 
-In R. Negative binomial regression of citation count on conclusion direction.
+Classification is therefore treated as an instrument that must be validated before it is used:
+
+1. A stratified random sample of **300 abstracts is classified by the author** into
+   inadequate, adequate, mixed, or not applicable. This is the reference standard. A model
+   does not produce it, for the reason R01 already demonstrated: a model checking a rule
+   another model wrote found a real defect and still left agreement with a person unknown.
+2. Two models from different providers classify the same 300, blind to the author's labels and
+   to each other.
+3. Agreement is reported as Cohen's kappa against the reference standard and between models.
+4. **If neither model reaches kappa 0.70 against the author's labels, the study stops and the
+   negative methodological result is published.** Whether a language model can read the
+   direction of a scientific conclusion is itself worth knowing.
+5. Only above the threshold does classification extend to the rest, with the measured
+   misclassification rate carried into a sensitivity analysis.
+
+The threshold is written here, before any classification has been run. A threshold chosen after
+seeing the agreement is not a threshold.
+
+## 7. Analysis
+
+In R.
 
 ```r
-glm.nb(cited_by_count ~ direction + poly(years_since_pub, 2) +

+glm.nb(cited_by_count ~ direction + poly(years_since_pub, 2) + topic_block + journal_size,
 ```

-Age enters as a quadratic rather than an offset because citation accrual is not linear in time
-and the corpus spans 1965 to 2026. A quantile regression on the upper citation deciles is
-fitted alongside, since a mean effect and a tail effect answer different questions and the
-incentive argument lives in the tail.
+Age enters as a quadratic because citation accrual is not linear in time and the corpus spans
+1965 to 2026. Preregistered alongside: quantile regression at the 75th and 90th percentiles,
+and the same model with `direction` as measured by each classifier separately, so that a result
+that depends on which model read the abstracts is visible as such.
+
+## 8. Threats, and what happened to each
+
+| threat | what was done | removed or disclosed |
+|---|---|---|
+| Reverse causation | Not addressable in this design. A widely cited article may attract a negative framing rather than the other way round | Disclosed |
+| Topic confounds direction | Some topics almost never produce an adequate verdict, so adjusting for topic may absorb the effect. Reported adjusted and unadjusted, with the difference discussed rather than resolved | Disclosed |
+| Classifier error | Gated on kappa against the author's labels, with a stated stop | Disclosed |
+| Citation counts move | Frozen at a dated snapshot in the repository | Removed |
+| Self-citation and field size | Not in the primary model; OpenAlex exposes the data and it is a stated secondary | Disclosed |
+| Zero-citation articles | Retained; dropping them conditions on the outcome | Removed |
 
-## Novelty
+## 9. Probes
 
-Predictors of citation rates have been reviewed (PMID 41265666) and citation bias is an
-established topic. What is absent is the direction of a study's own conclusion as the exposure,
-inside meta-research, where the conclusion direction is a property of the field's product
-rather than of a clinical result.
+| probe | computes | goes to | what would change the design | status |
+|---|---|---|---|---|
+| **PA** | OpenAlex match rate and citation distribution | 4, 5 | a match rate below about 90 percent makes the sample unrepresentative | done |
+| **PB** | classifier agreement against 300 author labels | 6 | below kappa 0.70 stops the study | outstanding, needs the author |
+| **PC** | detectable rate ratio from the realized exposure split | 7 | computed once PB returns, because it depends on how many articles fall in each direction | outstanding, depends on PB |
 
-## What it costs
+## 10. Deviations
 
-37 OpenAlex calls, free and unauthenticated. Hand classification of 300 abstracts is the real
-cost and it falls on the author, not on a model. Two model classification passes.
+Recorded on the result page: what changed, when, why, and the estimate before and after.
 
-## Known threats to the result
+## 11. What this cannot show
 
-- **Reverse causation is not addressable here.** A widely cited article may attract a negative
-  framing in the abstract rather than the other way round.
-- **Topic confounds direction.** Some topics almost never produce an adequate verdict, so
-  adjustment for topic block may absorb the effect being measured. Reported both adjusted and
-  unadjusted, with the difference discussed rather than resolved.
-- **Self-citation and field size.** Not adjusted for in the primary model. OpenAlex exposes the
-  data to do it and it is a stated secondary analysis.
+- It cannot show that negative findings are rewarded, only that they are cited more.
+- It cannot separate the effect of the finding from the effect of the topic that produced it.
+- OpenAlex citation counts include self-citations and citations from any source it indexes,
+  which is broader than PubMed and not identical to it.
